Antitrust liability may arise in a wide variety of circumstances including medical staff privilege disputes,
payor contracting, physician relations, joint ventures, merger, affiliation and acquisition activities, and
certain pricing and salary setting activities. Actions can be brought by federal and state enforcement
agencies seeking criminal and civil penalties and, in some instances, by private litigants seeking damages
for harm arising out of allegedly anti-competitive behavior. Common areas of potential liability include
joint action among providers with respect to payor contracting, medical staff credentialing and issues
relating to market share. Liability in any of these or other trade regulation areas may be substantial,
depending on the facts and circumstances of each case.



                                                    40
         Prompted by ACA, which promotes the formation of accountable care organizations among
otherwise independent providers and provider groups, the U.S. Federal Trade Commission and U.S.
Department of Justice issued a policy statement on October 28, 2011. The policy statement outlines,
among other things, a rule of reason approach to whether a collaboration through these organizations is
likely to have anticompetitive effects, and, if so, whether the collaboration’s potential procompetitive
efficiencies are likely to outweigh these effects.

         Some judicial decisions have permitted physicians who are subject to disciplinary or other
adverse actions by a hospital at which they practice, including denial or revocation of medical staff
privileges, to seek treble damages from the hospital under the federal antitrust laws. The Federal Health
Care Quality Improvement Act of 1986 provides immunity from liability for discipline of physicians by
hospitals under certain circumstances, but courts have differed over the nature and scope of this
immunity. In addition, hospitals occasionally indemnify medical staff members who incur costs as
defendants in lawsuits involving medical staff privilege decisions. Some court decisions have also
permitted recovery by competitors claiming harm from a hospital’s use of its market power to obtain
unfair competitive advantage in expanding into ancillary health care businesses. Antitrust liability in any
of these contexts can be substantial, depending upon the facts and circumstances involved. There can be
no assurance that a third party reviewing the activities of the Medical Center would find such activities to
be in full compliance with the antitrust laws.

Environmental Matters

         Health care providers are subject to a wide variety of federal, state and local environmental and
occupational health and safety laws and regulations. These requirements govern medical and toxic or
hazardous waste management, air and water quality control, notices to employees and the public, and
training requirements for employees. As an owner and operator of properties and facilities, the Medical
Center may be subject to potentially material liability for costs of investigating and remediating the
release of any such substances either on, or that have migrated off, the property, or that have been
improperly disposed of off-site. Typical health care provider operations include, but are not limited to, in
various combinations, the handling, use, storage, transportation, disposal and/or discharge of hazardous,
infectious, toxic, radioactive, flammable and other hazardous materials, medical and other wastes,
pollutants or contaminants. As such, health care provider operations are particularly susceptible to the
practical, financial and legal risks associated with the obligations imposed by applicable environmental
laws and regulations. Such risks may result in damage to individuals, property or the environment; may
interrupt operations and/or increase their cost; may result in legal liability, damages, injunctions or fines;
may result in investigations, administrative proceedings, civil litigation, criminal prosecution, penalties or
other governmental agency actions; and may not be covered by insurance. There can be no assurance that
the Medical Center will not encounter such risks in the future, and such risks may result in material
adverse consequences to the operations or financial condition of the Medical Center.

Affiliation, Merger, Acquisition and Divestiture

         As part of its ongoing planning and property management functions, the Medical Center reviews
the use, compatibility and financial viability of many of its operations, and from time to time, may pursue
changes in the use, or disposition, of its facilities. Likewise, the Medical Center may receive offers from,
or conduct discussions with, third parties about the potential acquisition of operations or properties that
may become part of the Medical Center in the future, or about the potential sale of some of the operations
and properties of the Medical Center. Discussions with respect to affiliation, merger, acquisition,
disposition, or change of use, including those that may affect the Medical Center, are held on an
intermittent, and usually confidential basis. As a result, it is possible that the assets currently owned by
the Medical Center may change from time to time, subject to the provisions in the financing documents
that apply to merger, sale, disposition or purchase of assets.

                                                     41
Insurance

         The dollar amounts of patient damage recoveries remain potentially significant. A number of
insurance carriers have withdrawn from this segment of the insurance market citing underwriting losses,
and premiums have increased in the last several years. The effect of these developments has been to
significantly increase the operating costs of hospitals, including the Medical Center.

          The Medical Center currently carries malpractice, directors’ and officers’ liability and general
liability insurance, which management of the Medical Center considers adequate, but no assurance can be
given that the Medical Center will maintain coverage amounts currently in place in the future, that the
coverage will be sufficient to cover all malpractice judgments rendered against the Medical Center or
settlements of any such claims or that such coverage will be available at a reasonable cost in the future.
For a discussion of the insurance coverage of the Medical Center, see “APPENDIX A – MAIMONIDES
MEDICAL CENTER – Insurance” herein.

Litigation and Claims

        The Medical Center is involved in litigation and claims which are not considered unusual to their
business. While the ultimate outcome of these lawsuits cannot be determined at this time, it is the opinion
of management that the ultimate resolution of these claims will not have a material adverse effect on the
Medical Center. The Medical Center, like most other employers in the current economic climate, has
experienced a recent increase in the number of employment-related actions. See “APPENDIX A –
MAIMONIDES MEDICAL CENTER – Litigation” and “– Material Pending Audits” herein.

Risks Related to Interest Rate Swap Agreements

        The Medical Center has entered into interest rate swap agreements related to indebtedness of the
Medical Center (the “Swaps”). The Swaps are and will be subject to periodic “mark-to-market”
valuations and at any time may have a negative value to the Medical Center. The Swap counterparties
may terminate the Swaps upon the occurrence of certain “termination events” or “events of default.” The
Medical Center may terminate the Swaps at any time. If either the counterparty to one of the Swaps or
the Medical Center terminates any of the Swaps during a negative value situation, the Medical Center
may be required to make a termination payment to such Swaps counterparty, and such payment could be
material. In addition, failure of the Swap counterparties to perform could lead to the Medical Center
bearing the risk of variable rate interest rate fluctuations. See “APPENDIX A – MAIMONIDES
MEDICAL CENTER – Discussion of Outstanding Indebtedness – Interest Rate Swaps” herein.

Bankruptcy

         The Series 2013 Bonds are payable from the sources and are secured as described in this Official
Statement. The practical realization of value from the collateral for the Series 2013 Bonds described
herein upon any default will depend upon the exercise of various remedies specified by the Indenture, the
Financing Agreement and the Mortgages. These and other remedies may, in many respects, require
judicial actions which are often subject to discretion and delay.

        Under existing law, the remedies specified by the Indenture, the Financing Agreement and the
Mortgages may not be readily available or may be limited. A court may decide not to order the
performance of the covenants contained in those documents. The legal opinions to be delivered
concurrently with the delivery of the Series 2013 Bonds will be qualified as to the enforceability of the
various agreements and other instruments by limitations imposed by State and federal laws, rulings and
decisions affecting remedies and by bankruptcy, reorganization or other laws affecting the enforcement of
creditors’ rights generally.

                                                    42
         The rights and remedies of the holders of the Series 2013 Bonds are subject to various provisions
of Title 11 of the United States Code (the “Bankruptcy Code”). If the Medical Center were to file a
petition for relief under the Bankruptcy Code, the filing would automatically stay the commencement or
continuation of any judicial or other proceedings against the Medical Center and its property, including
the commencement of foreclosure proceedings under the Mortgages. The Medical Center would not be
permitted or required to make payments of principal or interest under the Mortgage Notes, unless an order
of the United States Bankruptcy Court were issued for such purpose. In addition, without an order of the
United States Bankruptcy Court the automatic stay may serve to prevent the Trustee from applying
amounts on deposit in certain funds and accounts held under the Indenture from being applied in
accordance with the provisions of the Indenture, and the application of such amounts to the payment of
principal and Sinking Fund Installments of, and interest on, the Series 2013 Bonds. Moreover, any
motion for an order canceling the automatic stay and permitting such funds and accounts to be applied in
accordance with the provisions of the Indenture would be subject to the discretion of the United States
Bankruptcy Court, and may be subject to objection and/or comment by other creditors of the Medical
Center, which could affect the likelihood or timing of obtaining such relief. The automatic stay may also
extinguish the Trustee’s continuing security interest in the Medical Center’s revenues arising subsequent
to the filing of the bankruptcy petition, adversely affect the ability of the Trustee to exercise remedies
upon default, including the acceleration of all amounts payable by the Medical Center under the
Indenture, the Financing Agreement and the Mortgages, and may adversely affect the Trustee’s ability to
take all steps necessary to file a claim under the applicable documents on a timely basis.

         The Medical Center could file a plan for the adjustment of its debts in a proceeding under the
Bankruptcy Code, which plan could include provisions modifying or altering the rights of creditors
generally, or any class of them, whether secured or unsecured. The plan, when confirmed by the United
States Bankruptcy Court, would bind all creditors who have notice or knowledge of the plan and would
discharge all claims against the Medical Center provided for in the plan. No plan may be confirmed
unless certain conditions are met, among which are that the plan is in the best interests of creditors, is
feasible and has been accepted by each class of claims impaired thereunder. Each class of claims has
accepted the plan if at least two-thirds in dollar amount and more than one-half in number of the allowed
claims of the class that are voted with respect to the plan are cast in its favor. Even if the plan is not so
accepted, it may be confirmed if the court finds that the plan is fair and equitable with respect to each
class of non-accepting creditors impaired thereunder and does not discriminate unfairly.

         In the event of bankruptcy of the Medical Center, transfers of property by the bankrupt entity,
including the payment of debt or the transfer of any collateral, including receivables and revenues on or
after the date which is 90 days (or, in some circumstances, one year) prior to the commencement of the
case in bankruptcy court, may be subject to avoidance or recoupment as preferential transfers. Under
certain circumstances a court may have the power to direct the use of revenues to meet expenses of the
Medical Center before paying principal or interest on the Mortgage Notes.

Secondary Market

         There can be no assurance that there will be a secondary market for the purchase or sale of the
Series 2013 Bonds. From time to time there may be no market for them depending upon prevailing
market conditions, including the financial condition or market position of firms who may make the
secondary market, the evaluation of the Medical Center’s capabilities and the financial conditions and
results of operations of the Medical Center.




                                                     43
Other Risk Factors

        In the future, the following factors, among others, may adversely affect the operations of health
care providers, including the Medical Center, or the market value of the Series 2013 Bonds, to an extent
that cannot be determined at this time:

           (i)   Adoption of legislation that would establish a national or statewide single-payor health
                 program or that would establish national, statewide or otherwise regulated rates;

          (ii)   Increased unemployment or other economic conditions in the service area of the Medical
                 Center, which could increase the proportion of patients without health insurance benefits
                 or who are unable to pay fully for the cost of their care;

        (iii)    Continued efforts by insurers and governmental agencies to limit the cost of hospital and
                 physician services, to reduce the number of beds and to reduce the utilization of hospital
                 facilities by such means as preventive medicine, improved occupational health and safety
                 and outpatient care, or comparable regulations or attempts by third-party payors to
                 control or restrict the operations of certain health care facilities;

          (iv)   Efforts by employers to reduce the costs of health insurance by having employees bear a
                 greater portion of their health care costs, causing employees to be more selective and cost
                 conscious in choosing health care services;

          (v)    Reduced demand for the services of the Medical Center that might result from decreases
                 in population or innovations in technology;

          (vi)   Bankruptcy of an indemnity/commercial insurer, managed care plan or other payor; and

       (vii)     The occurrence of a natural or man-made disaster, including but not limited to acts of
                 terrorism, that could damage the facilities of the Medical Center, interrupt utility service
                 to the facilities, result in an abnormally high demand for health care services or otherwise
                 impair the operations and the generation of revenues from the Medical Center’s facilities.

Notice Pursuant to Treasury Department Circular 230

      TO ENSURE COMPLIANCE WITH TREASURY DEPARTMENT CIRCULAR 230,
PURCHASERS OF THE SERIES 2013 BONDS ARE HEREBY NOTIFIED THAT (1) ANY
DISCUSSION OF U.S. FEDERAL TAX ISSUES IN THIS OFFICIAL STATEMENT IS NOT
INTENDED OR WRITTEN TO BE RELIED UPON, AND CANNOT BE RELIED UPON BY A
PURCHASER, FOR THE PURPOSE OF AVOIDING PENALTIES THAT MAY BE IMPOSED ON A
PURCHASER UNDER THE INTERNAL REVENUE CODE OF 1986, AS AMENDED (THE
“CODE”), (2) SUCH DISCUSSION IS WRITTEN IN CONNECTION WITH THE PROMOTION OR
MARKETING OF THE TRANSACTIONS OR MATTERS ADDRESSED HEREIN AND (3) A
PURCHASER SHOULD SEEK ADVICE BASED ON ITS PARTICULAR CIRCUMSTANCES FROM
ITS OWN INDEPENDENT TAX ADVISORS.

General

       The following is a summary of certain U.S. federal income tax consequences relating to the
purchase, ownership and disposition of the Series 2013 Bonds. It does not provide a complete analysis of

                                                     44
all potential tax considerations relating to the purchase, ownership and disposition of the Series 2013
Bonds. This summary is based on the provisions of the Code, Treasury regulations promulgated or
proposed thereunder, judicial authority and administrative rulings and practice, all as of the date of this
Official Statement and all of which are subject to change, possibly retroactively, or to different
interpretation.

         This summary applies only to “holders” (as defined below) who purchase the Series 2013 Bonds
at their original issuance at their “issue price” within the meaning of Section 1273 of the Code and who
hold the Series 2013 Bonds as capital assets within the meaning of Section 1221 of the Code. A capital
asset generally is an asset held for investment rather than as inventory or as property used in a trade or
business. This summary does not discuss all of the aspects of U.S. federal income taxation that may be
relevant to investors in light of their particular investment or other circumstances. This summary also
does not discuss the particular tax consequences that might be relevant to investors that are subject to
special rules under the U.S. federal income tax laws, including: tax-exempt investors; foreign investors;
banks, thrifts, insurance companies, regulated investment companies or other financial institutions or
financial service companies; brokers or dealers in securities, commodities or foreign currencies; U.S.
persons that have a functional currency other than the U.S. dollar; U.S. persons that hold the Series 2013
Bonds through a foreign entity or foreign account; real estate investment trusts; financial asset
securitization investment trusts; subchapter S corporations; persons subject to alternative minimum tax;
persons who own the Series 2013 Bonds as part of a straddle, hedging transaction, constructive sale
transaction or other risk-reduction transaction; persons who have ceased to be U.S. citizens or to be taxed
as resident aliens; persons who acquire the Series 2013 Bonds in connection with their employment or
other performance of services; or entities or arrangements classified as partnerships for U.S. federal
income tax purposes or other pass-through entities and investors therein.

        The following summary does not address all possible tax consequences. In particular, it does not
discuss any estate, gift, generation skipping, or transfer tax consequences and it does not consider any tax
consequences arising under the laws of any foreign, state (except to the extent discussed under the
subheading “-State Taxation,” below), local or other jurisdiction. No ruling from the Internal Revenue
Service (the “IRS”) has been or will be sought with respect to the statements made and the conclusions
reached in the following summary, and there is no assurance that the IRS will agree with those statements
and conclusions. For all these reasons, each prospective investor is urged to consult its own tax advisor
with respect to the U.S. federal income tax and other tax consequences of the purchase, ownership and
disposition of the Series 2013 Bonds.

         As used herein, a “holder” is a beneficial owner of the Series 2013 Bonds who, for U.S. federal
income tax purposes, is (i) a citizen or individual resident of the United States, (ii) a corporation (or other
entity taxable as a corporation for U.S. federal income tax purposes) that is created or organized in or
under the laws of the United States, any state thereof or the District of Columbia, (iii) an estate the
income of which is subject to U.S. federal income taxation regardless of its source, or (iv) a trust if it (a)
is subject to the primary supervision of a court within the United States and one or more U.S. persons
have the authority to control all substantial decisions of the trust or (b) has a valid election in effect under
applicable Treasury regulations to be treated as a U.S. person.

        If a partnership, including an entity or arrangement treated as a partnership for U.S. federal
income tax purposes, is a holder of a Series 2013 Bond, the U.S. federal income tax treatment of a partner
in the partnership generally will depend on the status of the partner and the activities of the partnership.
Partnerships and their partners are urged to consult their own tax advisors as to the particular U.S. federal
income tax consequences applicable to them of an investment in the Series 2013 Bonds.




                                                      45
Tax Status of the Series 2013 Bonds

      THE SERIES 2013 BONDS ARE TAXABLE BONDS FOR U.S. FEDERAL INCOME TAX
PURPOSES. AS SUCH, INTEREST ON THE SERIES 2013 BONDS IS NOT EXCLUDABLE FROM
THE GROSS INCOME OF HOLDERS. SEE THE DISCUSSION BELOW FOR A MORE DETAILED
SUMMARY OF CERTAIN U.S. FEDERAL INCOME TAX CONSEQUENCES OF OWNING THE
SERIES 2013 BONDS.

Interest

         A holder of a Series 2013 Bond generally will be required to include in its gross income the stated
interest on a Series 2013 Bond at the time it is paid or accrued in accordance with the holder’s method of
accounting for U.S. federal income tax purposes. Such income will be treated as ordinary interest
income. It is anticipated, and the rest of this summary assumes, that the Series 2013 Bonds will be issued
without original issue discount or, if issued at a discount from the stated principal amount of the Series
2013 Bonds, with an amount of discount that is less than the statutory de minimis amount.

Sale, Exchange or Redemption of Series 2013 Bonds

         A holder generally will recognize gain or loss upon the sale, exchange or other taxable disposition
(including redemption or retirement) of a Series 2013 Bond measured by the difference between (i) the
amount of cash proceeds and the fair market value of any property received (except to the extent
attributable to accrued but unpaid interest) and (ii) the holder’s adjusted tax basis in such Series 2013
Bond. A holder’s adjusted tax basis in a Series 2013 Bond generally will equal its cost of the Series 2013
Bond, reduced by the amount of amortized premium. Any amount that is attributable to accrued but
unpaid interest, to the extent not previously included in income, will be taxable as interest. Gain or loss
on the disposition of Series 2013 Bonds generally will be a capital gain or loss and will be a long-term
capital gain or loss if the Series 2013 Bonds have been held for more than one year at the time of
disposition. Long-term capital gains of individuals and other non-corporate taxpayers generally are
eligible for preferential rates of taxation. The deductibility of capital losses is subject to limitations.
Prospective investors are urged to consult their own tax advisors regarding the treatment of capital gains
and losses.

Defeasance

        Defeasance of any Series 2013 Bond may result in a deemed disposition of such Series 2013
Bond and a deemed reissuance of a “new” Series 2013 Bond to the holder for U.S. federal income tax
purposes, in which case a holder would recognize taxable gain or loss equal to the difference between the
amount realized from the deemed exchange and the holder’s adjusted tax basis in the Series 2013 Bond.
The “new” Series 2013 Bond deemed reissued in such a defeasance may be treated as issued with original
issue discount in an amount equal to the excess, if any, of the stated redemption price at maturity of the
“new” Series 2013 Bond over its deemed issue price. Prospective investors are urged to consult their own
tax advisors regarding the tax consequences of a defeasance of the Series 2013 Bonds.

Additional Medicare Tax on Net Investment Income

         For taxable years beginning after December 31, 2012, certain holders who are individuals, estates,
or trusts and whose income exceeds certain thresholds will be subject to a Medicare tax on net investment
income. A holder's net investment income will generally include its gross interest income and its net
gains from the disposition of the Series 2013 Bonds, unless such interest or net gains are derived in the
ordinary course of the conduct of a trade or business (other than a trade or business that consists of certain
passive or trading activities). Prospective investors that are individuals, estates, or trusts are urged to

                                                     46
consult their own tax advisor regarding the applicability of the Medicare tax to the income and gains in
respect of an investment in the Series 2013 Bonds.

Information Reporting and Backup Withholding Tax

         In general, information reporting requirements will apply to payments to certain holders of
principal and interest on a Series 2013 Bond and the proceeds of a sale, exchange or other taxable
disposition (including a retirement or redemption) of a Series 2013 Bond. A holder may be subject to
backup withholding on such payments. In general, a holder can avoid this backup withholding by
properly executing under penalties of perjury an IRS Form W-9 or substantially similar form that
provides (1) such holder’s correct taxpayer identification number; and (2) a certification that such holder
(a) is exempt from backup withholding, (b) has not been notified by the IRS that it is subject to backup
withholding, or (c) has been notified by the IRS that it is no longer subject to backup withholding.

        Backup withholding does not apply to payments made to certain holders, provided their
exemptions from backup withholding are properly established. Backup withholding is not an additional
tax. Any amount withheld from a payment to a holder under the backup withholding rules will generally
be allowed as a credit against such holder’s U.S. federal income tax liability and may entitle such holder
to a refund, provided that the required information is timely furnished to the IRS. The amount of any
“reportable payments” for each calendar year and the amount of tax withheld, if any, with respect to those
payments will be reported to the holders of Series 2013 Bonds and to the IRS. Prospective investors are
urged to consult their own tax advisors regarding the application of backup withholding rules in their
particular situations, the availability of an exemption from backup withholding and the procedure for
obtaining such an exemption, if applicable.

State Taxation

        Interest on the Series 2013 Bonds and gain from the sale, exchange or other taxable disposition of
a Series 2013 Bond is not exempt from present New York taxation. Purchase, ownership and disposition
of a Series 2013 Bond may result in other state and local tax consequences to certain prospective
purchasers. Prospective purchasers of the Series 2013 Bonds are urged to consult their own tax advisors
concerning any specific state and local tax consequences.

      THE PRECEDING SUMMARY OF CERTAIN U.S. FEDERAL INCOME TAX
CONSIDERATIONS IS FOR GENERAL INFORMATION ONLY AND IS NOT LEGAL OR TAX
ADVICE. ACCORDINGLY, PROSPECTIVE INVESTORS SHOULD CONSULT THEIR OWN
ADVISORS REGARDING THE U.S. FEDERAL, STATE AND LOCAL, AND FOREIGN TAX
CONSEQUENCES OF THEIR PURCHASE, OWNERSHIP AND DISPOSITION OF THE SERIES
2013 BONDS, AND ON THE CONSEQUENCES OF ANY CHANGES IN APPLICABLE LAW.

MAIMONIDES MEDICAL CENTER
[This page intentionally left blank]
                            MAIMONIDES MEDICAL CENTER

Introduction
       Maimonides Medical Center (“Maimonides” or the “Medical Center”) is a voluntary, not-
for-profit, teaching hospital located in the Borough Park section of southwestern Brooklyn in
Kings County, New York, and is licensed to operate 711 beds. Maimonides is one of the largest
independent teaching hospitals in the United States. The Medical Center is an organization
exempt from federal income tax pursuant to Section 501(c)(3) of the Internal Revenue Code of
1986, as amended (the “Code”).

        The Medical Center was founded in 1911, absorbed several smaller dispensaries and, in
1919, become Israel Hospital of Brooklyn. In 1920, it merged with Zion Hospital and became
United Israel Zion Hospital. Maimonides Hospital traces its roots to a 1947 merger between
Beth Moses Hospital and United Israel Zion Hospital. Its status as a teaching hospital was
established in 1948, and in 1966 its name was changed to Maimonides Medical Center to reflect
the broad spectrum and comprehensive nature of the research, educational and medical activities
being performed.

       Today, the Medical Center provides a comprehensive array of acute care inpatient
services to residents of southern Brooklyn and tertiary care inpatient services to the entire
Borough of Brooklyn and surrounding metropolitan area. With a medical staff numbering 1,226
(as of December 31, 2011) and graduate medical residents numbering 480 (as of June 2012),
Maimonides serves as a teaching affiliate of the Albert Einstein College of Medicine
(“Einstein”), located in Bronx, New York, NYU School of Medicine, located in Manhattan, and
SUNY Downstate College of Medicine, located in Brooklyn, New York. Under a recently
signed affiliation agreement, Maimonides will become a university hospital and the Brooklyn
campus of Einstein.

         On February 26, 2013, Maimonides announced in a press release that it had entered into
an initial three year strategic alliance agreement with Montefiore Medical Center (“Montefiore”)
located in The Bronx, New York, to leverage the strengths of both institutions and to allow both
institutions to better prepare for new models of health care delivery in the future by establishing
programs of clinical excellence, expanding research capabilities and ensuring patient access to
the full range of clinical services – from patient care to advanced specialty care. Maimonides
and Montefiore will remain independent; each will continue to be solely responsible for its own
assets, operations and liabilities; and neither Maimonides nor Montefiore will incur any legal
responsibility for the liabilities and expenses of the other institution.

        For 2013, Maimonides made America’s 100 Best Hospitals list as determined by
HealthGrades, an independent ratings organization. The Medical Center was one of only three
New York State hospitals/hospital systems that received this award. In 2010, 2011, 2012 and
2013, the Medical Center received the Distinguished Hospital Award for Clinical Excellence
from HealthGrades. Maimonides was one of only two New York City hospitals/hospital systems
that received this award in 2013.

       The Medical Center continues to be a recognized national leader in the provision of
cardiac-related services, having been the recipient of the Cardiac Care Excellence Award from


                                               A-1
HealthGrades for the past three years, as one of only a few New York City hospitals so awarded
and recognized by the Department of Health for being one of the few hospitals in New York
State that achieved statistically significant excellent results in interventional cardiology.
Maimonides was also awarded the HealthGrades’ Coronary Interventional Excellence Award
and Women’s Health Excellence Award for the past three years, the Gastrointestinal Care
Excellence Award for 2013, and the Pulmonary Care Excellence Award and Stroke Care
Excellence Award for 2011 and 2012. Recently, Maimonides was ranked among the top
hospitals in Brooklyn (recipient of Modern Healthcare’s 2011-2012 Consumer Choice Award)
for overall quality and image. In addition, the Medical Center’s Left Ventricular Assist Device
(LVAD) Program, the only such program serving Brooklyn and Staten Island, was accredited by
The Joint Commission in 2012. See “Centers of Excellence” below.

Facilities
       Maimonides is located on a 6.67 acre campus at 4802 Tenth Avenue in the Borough Park
section of Brooklyn. The Medical Center’s campus consists of two city blocks, bounded by
Ninth Avenue and Fort Hamilton Parkway between 47th and 49th Streets.

       The main hospital campus consists of four inpatient buildings and numerous ambulatory
and ancillary buildings totaling approximately 1,000,000 square feet. Visitor and staff parking is
provided in a separate, six-level parking garage consisting of approximately 200,000 square feet.

        In 2009, a new building was placed into service by the Medical Center as part of its
$100+ million modernization program. The modernization program relocated selected inpatient
services from the Gellman, Kronish and Aron Buildings providing much needed expansion space
for existing acute care services, simplifying service delivery to patients through consolidation of
services and upgrading clinical departments. Specifically, this modernization program resulted
in an enlargement of both ambulatory and inpatient surgery facilities, new and improved state-of-
the-art medical technologies, and the reorganization and enlargement of three of Maimonides’
centers of excellence – the Cardiac Institute, the Infants and Children’s Hospital, and the
Emergency Department.

       In 2011, the freestanding Cancer Center, located approximately one mile from the
Maimonides main campus, was enhanced with the addition of the Breast Cancer Center which
provides state-of-the-art multi-modality imaging of the breast and surgical consultive services.

Services and Programs
       Maimonides offers a broad range of diagnostic and therapeutic services for adults and
children on an inpatient and outpatient basis. As of December 31, 2011, its certified bed
complement (excluding bassinets) was allocated among the following services:

                                Psychiatric                                  70

        The Medical Center’s comprehensive range of specialties and subspecialties includes:
anesthesiology, breast surgery, cardiology, cardiothoracic surgery, colorectal surgery, critical
care medicine, dentistry, dermatology, diagnostic radiology, emergency services, endocrinology,
fetal medicine, gastroenterology, general surgery, geriatrics, hematology, immunology,
infectious diseases, internal medicine, interventional cardiology, interventional neuroradiology,
laboratory medicine, medical genetics, metabolic diseases, neonatology, nephrology, neurology,
neurosurgery, nuclear medicine, obstetrics and gynecology, oncology, ophthalmology, oral
surgery, orthopedic surgery, otolaryngology, pathology, pediatrics and pediatric medical and
surgical subspecialties, surgery, podiatry, psychiatry, pulmonary diseases, radiation oncology,
rehabilitation medicine, reproductive endocrinology, rheumatology, thoracic surgery,
urogynecology, urologic oncology, urology and vascular surgery.

       In addition, Maimonides provides acute renal dialysis, ambulatory surgery, audiology,
cardiac catheterization, cystoscopy, family planning, geriatric day care, outpatient dentistry,
outpatient psychiatry, physical medicine and rehabilitation, physical therapy, primary medical
care services and social work services at various outreach sites throughout Brooklyn. These
outreach sites are primarily neighborhood-based clinics owned and operated by the Medical
Center as well as offices of physicians affiliated with the Medical Center.

Licensure and Accreditation
        Maimonides has operating certificates from the New York State Department of Health
Office of Health Systems Management and is accredited by The Joint Commission for a three
year period commencing February 11, 2012 and was accredited by The Joint Commission for its
Stroke Program, high technology home care agency and, most recently, for its LVAD Program in
2012. Maimonides is certified by the U.S. Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (“CMS”)
for participation in the Medicare and Medicaid programs.

Community Outreach Programs
       The Medical Center strives to provide exceptional, personalized care to its diverse
communities by extending its medical services into the surrounding neighborhoods. Its thirteen
(13) ambulatory health care facilities make primary and specialty care more accessible to all

                                                       A-3
residents of Brooklyn. In addition to offering primary care, many of the Medical Center’s
facilities offer specialty services. These ambulatory medical care facilities are fully equipped
with state-of-the-art information technology to allow streamlined communication with the
Medical Center and to provide quick access to laboratory and radiology reports. In addition, the
Maimonides network of ambulatory facilities is neighborhood-based and organized to meet the
language and cultural needs of its patients. Primary care services are targeted to specific
segments of the community, such as the elderly, who comprise the highest percentage of clinic
patients. The primary care centers are designated Level 3 Patient Centered and Medical Homes
by the National Committee for Quality Assurance (NCQA).

        In addition, the Medical Center welcomes new, emerging immigrant populations in
Brooklyn, including Asian, Caribbean, Pakistani and others via the “We Speak Your Language”
campaign. Medicaid patients constitute approximately 78% of clinic patients and 58% of the
patients using the outpatient services of the Community Mental Health Center.

        Examples of the Medical Center’s community outreach programs include a physician
referral service, which enables patients to locate physician services or specific medical care
services in their local community. A speaker’s bureau reaches out to local community
organizations and groups with prominent physicians who educate consumers on various current
topics in health promotion and treatment. Neighborhood-based health fairs and screenings are
conducted in schools, parks and community centers. A cardiac rehabilitation and exercise
program provides EKG-monitored exercises guided by specialists and lectures by cardiac-care
physicians, diagnostic screening examinations at community fairs and interactive self-
management programs for patients with cardiovascular disease and their families. The
Community Residence Support Program, a case-management program, coordinates medical,
psychiatric and entitlement programs, and assists mentally ill adults living in shelters, supportive
apartments and supervised residences.

Organization and Affiliates
       The following is a description of the affiliated corporations under common control with
or by the Medical Center, and its related organizations. Any payments made by the Medical
Center to these affiliated corporations are at fair market value for services rendered to the
Medical Center.

       MAIMONIDES HEALTH RESOURCES, INC.:                          A not-for-profit corporation
organized under the laws of the State of New York, a §501(c)(3) organization under the Code
and a supporting organization under §509(a)(3) of the Code, it is the holding company for
Maimonides and MMC Holding of Brooklyn, Inc. and is organized and operated to support the
Medical Center’s hospital, educational, scientific and charitable purposes. Maimonides Health
Resources, Inc. is the sole corporate member of the Medical Center.

        MAIMONIDES RESEARCH AND DEVELOPMENT FOUNDATION (the
“Foundation”): A New York not-for-profit corporation, a §501(c)(3) organization under the
Code and a supporting organization under §509(a)(3) of the Code, it is organized and operated
exclusively for the educational, scientific, medical and charitable purposes carried on by the
Medical Center. Its by-laws require that one more than the number which constitutes a quorum
of the board of directors must consist of persons who are trustees of Maimonides. At the present


                                                A-4
time, the Foundation uses the interest and dividend income from its funds to provide research
grants to applicants from Maimonides’ medical staff.

        MMC HOLDING OF BROOKLYN, INC. (“MMC Holding”): A New York for-profit
corporation which owns 100% of the capital stock of, and acts as the executive and accounting
offices for, each of the following five (5) active for-profit subsidiaries:

       (i)     MMC PHARMACY, INC., which operates a retail pharmacy serving the
               Medical Center’s outpatients and employees, and the community at large;

       (ii)    MMC MANAGEMENT CORP., which provides real estate management
               services to the Medical Center, including eleven (11) apartment buildings,
               approximately 300,000 square feet of leased commercial space and seven (7)
               parking lots;

       (iii)   INFUSION OPTIONS, INC., which serves as a wholesale pharmacy and
               distributor of infusion therapy and nursing services, with related medical
               equipment, to patients living at home and hospice patients;

       (iv)    EXECUTIVE PHYSICIAN SYSTEMS, INC., which provides billing services
               to the Medical Center and affiliated physicians, and other clients; and

       (v)     DIRECT STAFFING SERVICES, INC., which provides personnel leasing
               services that includes human resources, payroll, benefits administration and
               accounting to the Medical Center, and affiliated entities and physicians.

        The salaries of the subsidiaries’ employees are paid through MMC Holding and allocated
to the respective subsidiary based on time worked. All benefit plans are in the name of MMC
Holding. Management fees charged to the subsidiaries are paid to MMC Holding, and executive
offices are shared by MMC Holding and the subsidiaries. Expenses incurred by the Medical
Center provided by MMC Holdings of Brooklyn, Inc. through its various subsidiaries, less
related revenues, was $19.8 million in 2009, $19.6 million in 2010 and $19.4 million in 2011.

        FIRST TO CARE HOME CARE, INC.: The Medical Center is a joint venture owner
of First to Care Home Care, Inc., a New York not-for-profit corporation providing home care
services, with Metropolitan Jewish Geriatric Corporation. In 2011, the Medical Center recorded
a loss of approximately $1.5 million as a result of its ownership in this joint venture. The
Medical Center entered into an agreement to (i) terminate the Medical Center’s funding
obligations effective immediately and (ii) divest the Medical Center’s interest in this joint
venture with Metropolitan Jewish Geriatric Corporation which is expected to be implemented
within the next twelve months.

       FOJP-RELATED ENTITIES: The Medical Center retains ownership in three captive
insurance companies affiliated with the Federation of Jewish Philanthropies of New York (see
“Insurance” below).

       OTHER RELATED ORGANIZATIONS: The Medical Center is one of 18 owner
sponsor hospitals of Healthfirst, a not-for-profit managed care organization. As of June 2012,
Maimonides serviced 3.2% of the Healthfirst member population. The Medical Center is one of

                                             A-5
  six members of the Brooklyn Health Information Exchange (BHIX), a New York not-for-profit
  corporation that supports the advancement of health care information technology and maintains a
  clinical information exchange for numerous hospitals, health centers and other providers of
  health care in Brooklyn. In addition, the Medical Center is one of six contributing members and
  is a managing member of Southwest Brooklyn Health Home LLC (see “Centers of Excellence –
  Health Home”).

          The Medical Center is also affiliated with MMC Community Horizons, Inc., a New York
  not-for-profit corporation, that provides consulting and administrative services without charge,
  primarily to entities that deliver health care on a not-for-profit basis with a focus on the Medical
  Center and its affiliated entities. MMC Community Horizons, Inc. is one of the managing
  members of, and has a 5% membership interest in, Brooklyn SC, L.L.C., an entity formed to own
  and operate an ambulatory surgical center in Brooklyn.

         For information concerning the Medical Center’s transactions with affiliates, see Note 8
  of the Audited Financial Statements of the Medical Center for the years ended December 31,
  2011 and 2010 included in Appendix B.

         None of the affiliated corporations are obligated with respect to payments under the
  Series 2013 Bonds, the Financing Agreement or the Mortgage, which are solely obligations of
  the Medical Center.

         In addition, the Medical Center has had, and continues to have, discussions with other
  health care institutions with respect to possible affiliations, joint ventures and other similar
  arrangements (also see “Educational Programs” below).

  Governance
          The Medical Center is governed by a Board of Trustees (the “Board”) consisting of not
  less than three nor more than fifty members who serve staggered one to three-year terms and are
  elected at the annual meeting. As of October 19, 2012, there were 32 trustees on the Board.
  Regular meetings of the Board are scheduled at least four times per year. Officers of the Board
  are elected at the Board’s annual meeting generally held in April of each year. Board members
  monitor Maimonides’ operations through a varied and active group of committees.

        The Board has eleven (11) standing committees, which are the Executive Committee,
  Audit and Legal Committee, Budget and Finance Committee, Compensation Committee,
  Development Committee, Investment Committee, Joint Conference Committee, Medical Matters
  & Quality of Care Committee, Nominating Committee, Physical Facilities and Planning
  Committee, and Religious Observance Committee. The Executive Committee may exercise all


                                                    A-7
powers of the Board. The Audit and Legal Committee is responsible for compliance matters for
the Medical Center (see “Compliance” below).

        A conflict of interest policy for all trustees, officers, employees and members of the
medical staff of the Medical Center requires any potential conflict of interest to be disclosed.
Any transaction with the Medical Center or its affiliates in which a Board member has a financial
interest must be disclosed to the Board.

       In addition, as part of the recently executed affiliation agreement with Yeshiva University
through its Albert Einstein College of Medicine, two individuals recommended by Einstein will
serve on the Maimonides Board and two individual recommended by Maimonides will serve on
the Einstein board. However, both Maimonides and Einstein will each continue to be
autonomous and governed independently by their respective governing bodies and
administrations; and will each remain independent corporations.

Management
        The senior management of the Medical Center consists of the President and Chief
Executive Officer, three (3) executive vice presidents, the Chief Medical Officer, fifteen (15)
senior vice presidents and several vice presidents, and are summarized as follows:

       Pamela S. Brier, M.P.H., President and Chief Executive Officer (67). Ms. Brier has been
President and Chief Executive Officer of the Medical Center since May 2003. Previously, Ms.
Brier served as Executive Vice President/Chief Operating Officer of the Medical Center since
May 1995. Prior to joining the Medical Center, Ms. Brier served in numerous capacities in the
New York City Health and Hospitals Corporation from 1977 through 1995, most recently as
Chief Executive Officer of Bellevue Hospital Center (1992-95) and Jacobi Medical Center
(formerly Bronx Municipal Hospital Center) (1989-92). From 1976 to 1977, Ms. Brier was an
Associate Management Analyst for the New York City Department of Health, and from 1973 to
1975 she was a Health Plan Analyst for the Kaiser Foundation Health Plan, Inc. She was
appointed by Mayor Bloomberg to the New York City Board of Health (2003-present) and the
New York City Workforce Investment Board, and currently serves on the New York City Board
of Corrections and the Fund for Public Health in New York. Ms. Brier is a Fellow of The New
York Academy of Medicine, and served as a member of the Community Service Board/New
York City Department of Health (1995-2000), the Commonwealth Fund Commission on
Women’s Health (1993-96) and the New York State Hospital Review and Planning Council
(1991-96). In 2009 and 2011, she was recognized by Crain’s New York Business as one of the
“50 Most Powerful Women in New York.” From 1996 to 2003, Ms. Brier had served as Chair of
the Board of Directors of Housing Works, Inc. and on the Board of the Primary Care
Development Corporation. Currently, she is Vice Chair of the Board of The New York eHealth
Collaborative (NYeC), a statewide public/private partnership organization devoted to
information technology promulgation and adoption, and a board member of the UNITE
HERE/Union Health Center.

       Ms. Brier also served as a member of the adjunct faculty of the Wagner School of Public
Service, New York University and has lectured at the Zicklin School of Business Executive
Program at Baruch College. She received her M.P.H. in Health Administration from the School
of Public Health, University of California, Los Angeles in 1971 and her B.A. degree from the
University of California, Berkeley in 1967.

                                               A-8
        Dominick Stanzione, Executive Vice President and Chief Operating Officer (61). Mr.
Stanzione joined the Medical Center in August 2011 as Executive Vice President and Chief
Operating Officer. Prior to joining the Medical Center, for a period of three (3) years, he served
as Interim President and Chief Restructuring Officer at Long Island College Hospital which
recently merged with State University of New York Downstate Medical Center. Previously, he
worked in senior roles at several hospital systems in the Bon Secours Health System, the Sisters
of Charity Health Care System, and the Catholic Medical Centers of Brooklyn and Queens. Mr.
Stanzione received a B.S. in Health Sciences in physical therapy at State University of New
York Downstate in 1976 and his Master of Public Administration degree at New York University
in 1981.

        Robert Naldi, Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer (63). Mr. Naldi
joined the Medical Center in 2001 as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer.
Prior to joining the Medical Center, he held the dual role of Senior Vice President, Finance of St.
Luke’s-Roosevelt Medical Center and Beth Israel Medical Center. Prior positions held at Beth
Israel Medical Center include Director of Financial Operations and Budget Director. He is a
Board member of HANYS Insurance Company. Mr. Naldi received his Master of Business
Administration degree from Fordham University Graduate School of Business in 1974 and his
B.S. degree in Accounting from Lehman College of the City University of New York in 1972.

         David I. Cohen, M.D., M.Sc., Executive Vice President for Clinical Affairs and
Affiliations, and Senior Vice Chairman of the Department of Medicine (64). Dr. Cohen joined
the Medical Center in 1998. In addition to his other positions, he is currently responsible for
Academic Affairs and the Medicaid Health Homes Program at the Medical Center. Prior to
joining the Medical Center, he served as the first full-time Medical Director at Bellevue Hospital
Center (1991-98), as Deputy Dean for Clinical Affairs at the City University of New York
Sophie Davis School of Biomedical Education (1990-91), as Vice President for Medical
Operations of the New York City Health and Hospitals Corporation (1988-90) and as Director of
Ambulatory Care and Associate Dean for Graduate Medical Education at Mt. Sinai Medical
Center (1986-88). In 1981, Dr. Cohen was appointed Chief of the Division of General Internal
Medicine at Cleveland Metropolitan General Hospital (1981-86). In this position, he helped to
develop the “firm system” as a model for patient care, clinical education and health services
research. Prior to this, he was a faculty member of Case Western Reserve University (1979-80)
and served as Special Assistant to the Medical Director of the Harvard Community Health Plan
(1980-81). Dr. Cohen received his B.A. degree in 1970 and his M.D. degree in 1974 from the
University of Pennsylvania, and his M.Sc. degree in Epidemiology and Health from McGill
University where he was a Robert Wood Johnson Clinical Scholar (1977-79). He completed his
residency in internal medicine at St. Luke’s Hospital Center in New York City and his clinic
research fellowship at the Royal Victoria Hospital in Montreal.

        Samuel Kopel, M.D., Chief Medical Officer (67). In 1995, Dr. Kopel assumed the
position of Chief Medical Officer of Maimonides; previously, he served as the Medical Director
of Maimonides for six years and also as Associate Director of the Division of
Hematology/Oncology of Maimonides. Since 1978, he has been an Assistant Professor of
Clinical Medicine at SUNY Downstate Medical Center. Dr. Kopel came to the Medical Center
from Mount Sinai Hospital in New York where he completed his fellowships in research
hematology and oncology. He also completed his internship, residency in internal medicine and
fellowship in clinical hematology at the Jewish Hospital and Medical Center (now Kingsbrook

                                               A-9
Jewish Medical Center) in Brooklyn. Dr. Kopel received his B.A. degree from Brooklyn College
in 1967 and his M.D. from the University of Bologna, Italy in 1972. He is board certified in
hematology and medical oncology. Dr. Kopel is a member of the American Medical
Association, the American College of Physician Executives, The Medical Society of the State of
New York, The New York Academy of Sciences, as well as the American Society of Clinical
Oncology, the American Society of Hematology, the New York Society of Medical Oncologists
and Hematologists, The Society for Study of Blood and the World Federation of Hemophilia.

       Marcel Biberfeld, Senior Vice President – Psychiatry & Community Services (73). Dr.

and Vice President of the Medical Library, prior to his promotion in 1991 to his present position.
He is also the Vice President of Chaplaincy Services and oversees the Women, Infants and

of Directors at Ohel Children’s and Family Services, and Bikur Cholim/Guardians of the Sick of
Boro Park. Dr. Biberfeld received his B.A. degree from Brooklyn College in 1965, and his
Masters degree in Social Work in 1971 and his D.S.W. degree in 1983 from Yeshiva University.

        Martin A. Cammer, Senior Vice President and Chief Compliance Officer (62). Mr.
Cammer joined the Medical Center in 1980 as the Administrator of Community Medicine and
Ambulatory Services and has served in various administrative capacities during his tenure. Prior
to joining the Medical Center, Mr. Cammer was the Administrator for Ambulatory Care at
Jamaica Hospital and an Administrative Resident at LaGuardia Hospital. Mr. Cammer received
his M.B.A. degree in Hospital Administration from the Columbia University School of Business
Administration in 1977 and his B.A. degree from City College, City University of New York in
1972. Mr. Cammer is currently certified in Health Care Compliance (CHC).

        Steven J. Davidson, M.D., Senior Vice President and Chief Medical Informatics Officer
(62). Dr. Davidson’s position focuses on facilitating patient and patient-caregiver engagement in
their care through the use of mobile health and social media technologies, as well as supporting
clinical leaders in their use of information to evaluate and improve clinical processes, outcomes
and operational effectiveness. Prior to this position, he served for 15 years as founding
Chairman of Emergency Medicine at the Medical Center upon his arrival at the Medical Center
in 1995. He came to the Medical Center from the Medical College of Pennsylvania in
Philadelphia where he served his residency and trained in Emergency Medicine (1975-78) and
subsequently served on the faculty becoming Professor of Emergency Medicine (with tenure).
Dr. Davidson received his AB degree in Chemistry from Temple University in 1971, his M.D.
from Temple University School of Medicine in 1975 and his MBA from The Wharton School of
the University of Pennsylvania in 1989.

       Tom Doherty, Senior Vice President for Human Resources (66). Mr. Doherty joined the
Medical Center in January 2012. From 1996 until joining the Medical Center, he provided
management consulting services in the human resources area to various hospitals and healthcare
organizations which included serving as a labor mediator appointed by both Local 1199SEIU
and the Catholic Medical Center of Brooklyn and Queens to mediate all labor disputes, and as a
labor arbitrator for the New York City Transit Authority and the Transit Workers Union in

                                              A-10
disciplinary proceedings. Mr. Doherty previously spent 15 years at the New York City Heath
and Hospitals Corporation where he held human and labor-related positions, including Senior
Vice President for Administration, Vice President for Human Resources and Assistant Vice
President of Labor Relations. He attended Manhattan College and received a certificate in labor
relations from the School of Industrial and Labor Relations at Cornell University in 1981.

       Walter J. Fahey, Senior Vice President and Chief Information Officer (64). As Chief
Information Officer, Mr. Fahey’s responsibilities include information technology and
telecommunications at Maimonides. In this regard, he helped Maimonides earn accolades for
successful health care system implementation and compliance (see “Centers of Excellence –
Health Care Information Technology”). He was also instrumental in the development of the
Heal 1, 5, 10 and 17 Grant applications to develop the Brooklyn Health Information Exchange
(BHIX). Mr. Fahey arrived at Maimonides in 1995 from The Hospital for Joint Diseases where
he worked as Director of Management Information Systems from 1991 to 1995. He also held
information technology leadership positions at St. Vincent’s Hospital in Manhattan (1990-91)
and The New York Methodist Hospital (1986-90). Earlier in his professional information
technology career, he worked in manufacturing, retail and computer services. Mr. Fahey is a
Board Member of the American Health Association, and a member of the Allscripts Executive
Advisory Group, the Aruba Advisory Group, the College of Healthcare Information
Management Executives (CHIME), the Gershon Lehrman Group Councils of Advisors, the
Healthcare Association of New York State (HANYS), the Healthcare Information and
Management Systems Society (HIMSS), the HIT Strategy Group, the Unisys Advisory Group
and the Verizon Heathcare Advisory Group, and a former member of the NextGen Advisory
Board. Mr. Fahey received his MBA from Farleigh Dickinson University (New Jersey) in 2001.

       Lillian Fraidkin, Senior Vice President and Chief of Staff (74). Ms. Fraidkin joined the
Medical Center in 1982 as Associate Director of the Emergency Department and Assistant
Director of Nursing, and served as Administrative Director of the Operating Room, Post
Anesthesia Care Unit and the Emergency Department (1985-88) and Administrator of the
Emergency Room, Operating Room, Critical Care and Clinical Services, and Associate Director
of Nursing (1988-94) prior to appointment to her present position in 1994. Prior to joining
Maimonides, Ms. Fraidkin was in charge of the Emergency Department at Beth Israel Medical
Center in New York City from 1965 to 1982. Ms. Fraidkin earned her Nursing Diploma from
the Beth Israel School of Nursing, her B.A. degree from Marymount College in 1978 and her
M.S. degree in Health Care Administration from Long Island University in 1986.

        Derek D. Goins, Senior Vice President – Facilities & Support Services, and Vice
President of MMC Management Corp. (60). Mr. Goins joined the Medical Center in 1972 and
has served in various capacities, including Administrator of MMC Management Corp. (1989-
1991) and Assistant Director of Plant Management (1984-85). He was promoted to his present
position in 1991 and, as Vice President of MMC Management Corp., is responsible for the
management of all of the real estate holdings of the Medical Center. Mr. Goins attended St.
Joseph’s College for Hospital Administration and holds a Registered Apartment Manager
Certificate from New York University.

       Douglas Jablon, Senior Vice President of Patient Relations and Volunteer Services;
Special Assistant to President (62). In this position, Mr. Jablon focuses on the social, economic
and cultural needs of patients and their families, and oversees the activities of over 1,800

                                              A-11
volunteers and numerous volunteer programs surrounding the Medical Center. Mr. Jablon has a
36-year service history with the Medical Center and the community it serves. As an advocate for
patient care and patients’ rights and professional courteous customer service, Mr. Jablon is
involved in every aspect of care provided by the Medical Center and, in this role, acts as the
“sounding board” for patient, family and community concerns. He received his Associate of

Sociology from the Rabbinical Assembly in 1973 and his Master’s degree in Education,
Guidance and Counseling from Long Island University in 1977.

        Joyce A. Leahy, Esq., Senior Vice President for Legal Affairs and General Counsel;
Assistant Secretary (59). Ms. Leahy joined the Medical Center in 1987 as General Counsel. In
1991, she was appointed Assistant Secretary and her title was changed to Vice President for

Prior to joining the Medical Center, Ms. Leahy was an associate with the law firm of De Forest

University in 1975 and her J.D. degree from New York University School of Law in 1978. She
is a member of the Bars of New York and Massachusetts, and is admitted to practice before the
United States District Courts for the Southern and Eastern Districts of New York.

       Damiano Marino, Senior Vice President, Financial Operations/Controller (62). Mr.
Marino joined the Medical Center in 1998 and previously served as Vice President for
Finance/Controller and Assistant Vice President/Controller. Prior to his joining the Medical
Center, Mr. Marino served as Associate Director of Finance, Controller, and Senior Budget and
Reimbursement Accountant at Catholic Medical Center of Brooklyn and Queens (1980-98),
served as Budget and Reimbursement Accountant (1977-80) at Methodist Hospital and Staff
Accountant at Sterling Drugs (1975-77). Mr. Marino received his B.S. in Accounting from St.
Francis College in 1975.

        Sheila Namm, R.N., Senior Vice President for Professional Affairs (65). Ms. Namm’s
responsibilities in this position include organizational performance, risk management, medical
staff peer review, regulatory affairs, bioethics, credentialing, emergency management, safety,
patient safety, disclosure/apology, Joint Commission readiness and claims management. She
also leads the Bioethics Consultation Service at Maimonides. Ms. Namm joined the Medical

positions throughout the Medical Center, she was appointed Vice President for Professional
Affairs in 2001. Prior to joining the Medical Center, Ms. Namm was a Nurse Clinician at Kings
County Hospital for child and adolescent psychiatry. She received her B.S. degree in Nursing
from Hunter College in 1968, her M.A. degree in Child and Adolescent Mental Health Nursing
from New York University in 1970 and her J.D. degree from New York Law School in 1995, a
certificate in Bioethics and Medical Humanities from Montefiore Medical Center/Albert Einstein
School of Medicine in 2007 and a certificate in Just Culture from Outcomes Engineering in
2010. Ms. Namm is a member of the Bars of New York and New Jersey, and is licensed as a
registered nurse in New York State.

       Karen Nelson, M.D., M.P.H., Senior Vice President for Integrated Delivery Systems and
Executive Director of the Southwest Brooklyn Health Home (56). Dr. Nelson re-joined the
Medical Center staff in April 2012 where she had been the Vice Chair of Medicine from 1996 to

1999. Between 1999 and 2012, she was the Chief Executive Officer and Medical Director of the
Union Health Center which provides primary and specialty care to immigrant and low wage
union members and their families in New York City. She was the Medical Director of

School of Medicine in 1985. She received her Masters of Public Health from Boston University

1996.

        John Scanlan, Senior Vice President for Finance (50). In this position for the past four
(4) years, Mr. Scanlan oversees accounting, accounts payable, budget, chargemaster, decision
support/cost accounting, financial planning, managed care contracting, payroll and
reimbursement at the Medical Center. Since joining the Medical Center in 1997, he served as
Vice President for Finance, Associate Vice President for Finance and Director of
Reimbursement.      Mr. Scanlan served as Manager of Reimbursement, Supervisor of
Reimbursement and Staff Accountant at Catholic Medical Center of Brooklyn and Queens
(1985-97). He is currently a fellow in the Healthcare Financial Management Association
(HFMA) and is a Past President of the Metropolitan New York Chapter, who received the Medal
of Honor, the Muncie Gold, the Reeves Silver and the Follmer Bronze Founders Awards. He
also previously served as Vice President of Education, Treasurer and Secretary, and currently is
the Chair of the Audit, Certification Coaching and Continuing Professional Education
Committees of the HFMA Metropolitan New York Chapter. He has served as Chair of the
Finance and General Education Committees as well as the Joseph A. Levi Annual Institute for
the HFMA Metropolitan New York Chapter. Mr. Scanlan has served on the national Board of
Examiners of HFMA. He is currently serving on the Board of Directors of the Bay Ridge Center
as Finance Chair. Mr. Scanlan is also an Associate of the American College of Healthcare
Executives. Mr. Scanlan received his B.S. in Finance from St. John’s University in 1985 and his
MBA in healthcare administration from Adelphi University in 1990.

        Tom Smith, R.N., DNP, Senior Vice President – Nursing and Chief Nursing Officer (62).
Mr. Smith joined the Medical Center in this position in January 2010. At Maimonides, he is
responsible for nursing practice and patient care delivery. Mr. Smith was Chief Nursing Officer
and Senior Vice President at Cambridge Health Alliance (2006-10) and Senior Vice President for
Nursing at The Mount Sinai Hospital (1996-2005), the first full-service hospital in Manhattan to
receive the Magnet Award for nursing excellence, which was received during his tenure. Mr.
Smith has previously held leadership positions at Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston
Medical Center (formerly Boston City Hospital) and University of Massachusetts-Boston. He
has served as a Commissioner for the Commission on Collegiate Nursing Education (CCNE) of
the American Association of Colleges of Nursing and is an active member of the American
Organization of Nurse Executives. Mr. Smith received his B.S. in Nursing from the University
of the State of New York in 1980, his M.S. in cardiopulmonary nursing from Boston University
in 1985 and his Doctor of Nursing Practice (DNP) from Case Western Reserve University in
2010. He is a fellow in the American Academy of Nursing.

        Harold Wodinsky, Senior Vice President, Physician Enterprises (57). Mr. Wodinsky

       Eric Scalettar, Vice President – Planning (60). Mr. Scalettar joined the Medical Center

Binghamton in 1976.

Medical Staff
        As of December 31, 2011, there were 1,226 physician members on the medical staff of
the Medical Center holding appointments in three categories: employed (429), voluntary (777)
and honorary (20). In 2011, approximately 600 physicians admitted patients to Maimonides.
The medical staff is organized into 11 clinical departments. The Department of Medicine is
comprised of 13 clinical divisions: Allergy and Immunology, Cardiology, Dermatology,
Gastroenterology, Geriatric Medicine, Hematology/Oncology, Infectious Diseases, Internal
Medicine, Metabolic Endocrinology, Nephrology, Neurology, Pulmonary Diseases and Critical
Care, and Rheumatology and Immunology. The Department of Surgery is comprised of 12
clinical divisions:      Colorectal Surgery, Dentistry, General Surgery, Neurosurgery,
Ophthalmology, Otolaryngology, Pediatric Surgery, Plastic Surgery, Surgical Critical Care,
Thoracic and Cardiovascular Surgery, Urology, and Vascular Surgery. The Department of
Pediatrics is comprised of 14 clinical divisions: Adolescent Medicine, Cardiology, Critical Care,
Dermatology,       Endocrinology,      Gastroenterology,     General    Pediatrics,    Genetics,
Hematology/Oncology, Infectious Diseases, Neonatology, Nephrology, Neurology and
Pulmonary. The Department of Obstetrics and Gynecology has 3 clinical divisions:
Gynecology/Oncology, Maternal and Fetal Medicine, and Reproductive Endocrinology. The
Department of Orthopedic Surgery and Musculoskeletal Services has 3 clinical divisions:
Orthopedic Surgery, Podiatry and Rehabilitation Medicine. The Department of Psychiatry also
includes the Division of Psychology. The Department of Radiology is comprised of 9 clinical
divisions:     Body Imaging, Diagnostic Radiology, Emergency Radiology, Interventional
Radiology, Mammography, Musculoskeletal Radiology, Nuclear Medicine, Pediatric Radiology
and Radiotherapy. There are also Departments of Anesthesia, Emergency Medicine, Pathology
and Laboratory Medicine, and Radiation Oncology. In 2011, the average age of the medical staff
was 51 years old.

       The following is a summary by department of the number of physicians, their average
age and the percentage of department staff who are board certified as of December 31, 2011:

Nursing
        As of September 30, 2012, the Medical Center employed approximately 2,294 full-time
equivalent (“FTE”) employees on its nursing/patient care staff. This includes approximately
1,212 FTE registered nurses (excluding advanced practice nurses), 37 FTE licensed practical
nurses, 488 FTE patient care technicians/mental health workers, 124 FTE ward
clerks/information specialists, and 60 FTE nursing supervisors and administrators. An additional

                                                      A-15
373 FTE employees include advanced practice nurses, technicians from other areas (e.g.
operating room, heart monitoring and hemodialysis) and a variety of other specialty roles such as
nurse educators. In recent years, the Medical Center has been able to successfully recruit and
retain an adequate number of registered nurses despite periodic shortages and fluctuations in the
supply of registered nurses. The current vacancy rate of registered nurses is less than 2 percent.

Employees
        As of December 31, 2011, the Medical Center employed approximately 5,621 FTE
employees, which include 231 FTE employees related to specific purpose programs and
contracts. The number of FTE employees has increased in the last year by approximately 1.2%
as a result of expanded programs and higher acuity patients.

Employee Benefits
        The Medical Center offers to eligible employees health insurance covering
hospitalization, major medical expenses, prescription drugs, and dental, health care and
dependent care reimbursement accounts; short and long term disability; life insurance; pension
plan; and tuition reimbursement. Both the New York State Nurses Association Pension Plan and
the 1199 SEIU Health Care Employees Pension Fund are multiemployer defined benefit pension
plans (see “Pension Plans and Post-Retirement Health Care Benefits” below).

Collective Bargaining Agreements/Employee Relations
       Maimonides has three collective bargaining agreements covering approximately 71% of
its employees: an agreement with the Committee of Interns and Residents covers 434 residents
and fellows, and expires on October 31, 2013; an agreement with the New York State Nurses
Association covers approximately 1,281 registered nurses and expires on January 1, 2014; and an
agreement with Local 1199 covers approximately 2,703 service, clerical and technical employees
and expires on April 30, 2015. Maimonides considers its relations with its employees to be very
good.

Educational Programs
        Maimonides serves as a major teaching affiliate of the Albert Einstein College of
Medicine of Yeshiva University in The Bronx and the NYU School of Medicine in Manhattan,
and is also a teaching affiliate of the SUNY Downstate College of Medicine in Brooklyn.
Maimonides recently signed an affiliation agreement with Yeshiva University through Einstein
to strengthen Maimonides’ teaching programs and fortify physician recruitment at Maimonides.
Einstein operates one of the largest post-graduate medical training programs in the United States.
Maimonides physicians who teach and supervise Einstein students will receive faculty
appointments at Einstein, and will participate on faculty committees and in faculty development
programs.

       The Medical Center is a primary participant in three major integrated programs with
SUNY Downstate College of Medicine: Endocrinology, Otolaryngology, and Pediatric
Dermatology and Pediatric Endocrinology. Maimonides sponsors 22 residency and fellowship
programs fully accredited by the Accreditation Council for Graduate Medical Education
(ACGME). Maimonides also sponsors an Osteopathic Internship program fully accredited by
the American Osteopathic Association (AOA). As a direct result of this commitment to


                                              A-16
physician education, the Medical Center provides clinical training to approximately 480 residents
in numerous specialties and over 1,000 medical students.

        The Medical Center also partners with various social service, medical service, and
nursing home providers by entering into linkage or back-up medical services agreements wherein
the Medical Center agrees to make its facilities available as a back-up medical resource to the
patients or members of the other entity in cases where it is beyond the scope of such entity to
provide the contemplated medical services.

         In addition, the Medical Center maintains educational affiliations with numerous
institutions including, but not limited to, the following institutions: Bellevue Hospital Center,
Brookdale Medical Center, College of Mount Saint Vincent, College of New Rochelle,
Columbia University, Coney Island Hospital, Connecticut Children’s Medical Center, CUNY –
Borough of Manhattan Community College, CUNY – LaGuardia Community College, CUNY –
Kingsborough Community College, East Stroudsburg University of Pennsylvania, Emory
University, Excelsior College, Hartford Hospital, Hofstra University, Kaplan University, Kean
University, Kings County Hospital Center, Long Island University – C.W. Post, Lutheran
Medical Center, Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center, Midwood Dialysis Center, Molloy
College, Montefiore Medical Center – Henry and Lucy Moses Division, Mount Sinai Medical
Center, New York City Poison Control Center, North Shore – Long Island Jewish Health
System, NYU Langone Medical Center, Pace University, Quinnipiac University, Richmond
University Medical Center, SUNY – Canton, SUNY – Downstate Medical Center, Touro
College, University of Florida, University of Maryland Shock Trauma Center, Wagner College,
Walden University, Weber State University and Yale University.
